People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

I wasn’t sure if that was enough of a reason to come in, but it’s been bothering me.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Probably around eight months… maybe a little less at first, but that’s when I started noticing the whole shift in how I was thinking, working, and keeping up with people. It kind of began after we took my older relative into our apartment. I mean, I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but it’s been pretty steady since then.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods where you felt extremely down or depressed during these eight months?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’d call it extremely depressed, no. I’ve had low days, definitely… kind of flat or discouraged, but I’ve still been getting up and doing what I need to do. So, I don’t know if that counts? It hasn’t felt like a big depressive episode to me.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I don’t want to overstate it, because I still do some things when I have to, but the enjoyment part is much quieter. Work used to feel meaningful, and I used to look forward to yoga or going to galleries, even journaling. Lately it’s more like I’m just going through the motions, doing obligations rather than really wanting to. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past eight months?\n\nPatient: Not really, no… at least nothing significant that I’ve noticed. My clothes fit about the same, I think. I haven’t been tracking it closely, so I don’t want to give you a wrong answer, but I don’t think there’s been a major change.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any anxiety or feelings of worry?\n\nPatient: A little, maybe… but I wouldn’t say it’s been overwhelming. I do worry sometimes, probably more than I should, but I can usually keep it contained and still get through the day. I’m not sure if that’s the kind of anxiety you mean.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

I guess… yes and no. I can still appreciate things, sort of, but it’s much quieter than it used to be. Like with work, yoga, galleries, even journaling—I’ll still do them, but a lot of the time it feels like I’m just completing the obligation rather than really reaching for it or enjoying it.

Strong? Um… not really, if I’m being honest. I mean, I can still get through things and sometimes there are small moments that are nice, but I wouldn’t say there’s anything lately where I feel that strong pull or real enjoyment.

That’s kind of what finally made me come in. The part of me that used to reach for people, or feel connected to my work, yoga, galleries, even journaling… it’s just gotten very quiet. I’m mostly doing them because they’re things I’m supposed to do, or used to do, rather than because I’m really enjoying them. Does that make sense?

  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n10\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.0, F32.1, F33.0, F33.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi… thank you. I guess I’m here because I’ve been feeling kind of… muted, if that makes sense. It’s not dramatic, and I’m still doing what I need to do, but the part of me that used to reach out to people or really enjoy things—my work, yoga, going to galleries, even journaling—has just gotten very quiet. Mostly I feel like I’m moving through obligations. I wasn’t sure if that was enough of a reason to come in, but it’s been bothering me.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Probably around eight months… maybe a little less at first, but that’s when I started noticing the whole shift in how I was thinking, working, and keeping up with people. It kind of began after we took my older relative into our apartment. I mean, I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but it’s been pretty steady since then.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods where you felt extremely down or depressed during these eight months?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’d call it extremely depressed, no. I’ve had low days, definitely… kind of flat or discouraged, but I’ve still been getting up and doing what I need to do. So, I don’t know if that counts? It hasn’t felt like a big depressive episode to me.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I don’t want to overstate it, because I still do some things when I have to, but the enjoyment part is much quieter. Work used to feel meaningful, and I used to look forward to yoga or going to galleries, even journaling. Lately it’s more like I’m just going through the motions, doing obligations rather than really wanting to. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past eight months?\n\nPatient: Not really, no… at least nothing significant that I’ve noticed. My clothes fit about the same, I think. I haven’t been tracking it closely, so I don’t want to give you a wrong answer, but I don’t think there’s been a major change.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any anxiety or feelings of worry?\n\nPatient: A little, maybe… but I wouldn’t say it’s been overwhelming. I do worry sometimes, probably more than I should, but I can usually keep it contained and still get through the day. I’m not sure if that’s the kind of anxiety you mean.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts about hurting yourself or ending your life during this time?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. I mean, I’ve felt low and kind of worn down, but I haven’t been thinking about hurting myself or ending my life. I’d tell you if that changed. Is that okay to say?\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your low days have been frequent enough to be considered a regular part of your week?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it a regular part of my week… I mean, I do have low days here and there, but I haven’t really been counting them. It doesn’t feel like it’s every week in a definite way, if that makes sense. Maybe I should pay closer attention, though.\n\nDoctor: Do you still feel pleasure or enjoyment from the activities you're going through the motions with, even if it's not as strong as it used to be?\n\nPatient: I guess… yes and no. I can still appreciate things, sort of, but it’s much quieter than it used to be. Like with work, yoga, galleries, even journaling—I’ll still do them, but a lot of the time it feels like I’m just completing the obligation rather than really reaching for it or enjoying it.\n\nAnd with people too… I used to naturally want to connect more, and now that part of me feels like it’s gone kind of still. I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, because I’m functioning, but that’s actually part of why I came in. It feels like I’m moving through the motions more than feeling pleasure from things. Is that the kind of thing you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities where you still feel a strong sense of pleasure or enjoyment?\n\nPatient: Strong? Um… not really, if I’m being honest. I mean, I can still get through things and sometimes there are small moments that are nice, but I wouldn’t say there’s anything lately where I feel that strong pull or real enjoyment.\n\nThat’s kind of what finally made me come in. The part of me that used to reach for people, or feel connected to my work, yoga, galleries, even journaling… it’s just gotten very quiet. I’m mostly doing them because they’re things I’m supposed to do, or used to do, rather than because I’m really enjoying them. Does that make sense?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
A few small things, maybe… like a good cup of coffee, or if the light is nice when I’m walking somewhere. I can notice those things. I don’t want to make it sound like there’s nothing at all.

But I guess I don’t really look forward to much in the way I used to. The part of me that would reach for people, or feel excited about work, yoga, going to galleries, journaling… it’s just very quiet. I still do them, mostly because they’re good for me or because I’m supposed to, but it feels more like moving through obligations than actually taking pleasure in them. A little, yes… I mean, I’m still showing up. I don’t want to make it sound like I’ve completely fallen apart or anything, because I am going to work and getting through the day.

Yes… I think so, though I’d maybe describe it less as physical tiredness and more like… everything takes more effort than it should.

I can still do the basics, and I don’t want to overstate it, but most days I feel like I’m just moving through obligations. Maybe I should pay closer attention, though.\n\nDoctor: Do you still feel pleasure or enjoyment from the activities you're going through the motions with, even if it's not as strong as it used to be?\n\nPatient: I guess… yes and no. I can still appreciate things, sort of, but it’s much quieter than it used to be. Like with work, yoga, galleries, even journaling—I’ll still do them, but a lot of the time it feels like I’m just completing the obligation rather than really reaching for it or enjoying it.\n\nAnd with people too… I used to naturally want to connect more, and now that part of me feels like it’s gone kind of still. I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, because I’m functioning, but that’s actually part of why I came in. It feels like I’m moving through the motions more than feeling pleasure from things. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Are there any small pleasures in daily life that you still look forward to?\n\nPatient: A few small things, maybe… like a good cup of coffee, or if the light is nice when I’m walking somewhere. The part of me that would reach for people, or feel excited about work, yoga, going to galleries, journaling… it’s just very quiet. I still do them, mostly because they’re good for me or because I’m supposed to, but it feels more like moving through obligations than actually taking pleasure in them. Is that the sort of answer you mean?\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your current state is interfering with your ability to function in daily life?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… I mean, I’m still showing up. I don’t want to make it sound like I’ve completely fallen apart or anything, because I am going to work and getting through the day.\n\nBut… I’m not functioning at my usual level. I’ve missed some grant deadlines, which is embarrassing to say. And my supervisor has, very quietly, moved some of my more demanding clients to other clinicians. Does that answer it?\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing a lack of energy or fatigue on most days?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so, though I’d maybe describe it less as physical tiredness and more like… everything takes more effort than it should.\n\nI can still do the basics, and I don’t want to overstate it, but most days I feel like I’m just moving through obligations. Is that the sort of answer you mean?\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your current state is interfering with your ability to function in daily life?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… I mean, I’m still showing up. I don’t want to make it sound like I’ve completely fallen apart or anything, because I am going to work and getting through the day.\n\nBut… I’m not functioning at my usual level. I’ve missed some grant deadlines, which is embarrassing to say. And my supervisor has, very quietly, moved some of my more demanding clients to other clinicians. She didn’t make a big thing of it, but I know why it’s happening.\n\nSo yes, I guess it is interfering, even if I’m trying to keep it contained. Does that answer it?\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing a lack of energy or fatigue on most days?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so, though I’d maybe describe it less as physical tiredness and more like… everything takes more effort than it should.\n\nI can still do the basics, and I don’t want to overstate it, but most days I feel like I’m just moving through obligations. Work, yoga, seeing people, even things like galleries or journaling that used to give me something back… they’ve all gone kind of quiet for me. It’s like the part of me that used to reach for things doesn’t really come online.\n\nSo yes, there is a lack of energy, in that sense. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your feelings of being muted and going through the motions are present most of the day?\n\nPatient: Yes… most of the day, I think. I don’t mean I’m completely blank or anything — I can still do things, and sometimes I can notice something pleasant.\n\nBut that muted feeling is kind of the default right now. I do worry sometimes, probably more than I should, but I can usually keep it contained and still get through the day. Is that the sort of answer you mean?\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your current state is interfering with your ability to function in daily life?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… I mean, I’m still showing up. I don’t want to make it sound like I’ve completely fallen apart or anything, because I am going to work and getting through the day.\n\nBut… I’m not functioning at my usual level. I’ve missed some grant deadlines, which is embarrassing to say. And my supervisor has, very quietly, moved some of my more demanding clients to other clinicians. She didn’t make a big thing of it, but I know why it’s happening.\n\nSo yes, I guess it is interfering, even if I’m trying to keep it contained. Does that answer it?\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing a lack of energy or fatigue on most days?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so, though I’d maybe describe it less as physical tiredness and more like… everything takes more effort than it should.\n\nI can still do the basics, and I don’t want to overstate it, but most days I feel like I’m just moving through obligations. Work, yoga, seeing people, even things like galleries or journaling that used to give me something back… they’ve all gone kind of quiet for me. It’s like the part of me that used to reach for things doesn’t really come online.\n\nSo yes, there is a lack of energy, in that sense. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your feelings of being muted and going through the motions are present most of the day?\n\nPatient: Yes… most of the day, I think. I don’t mean I’m completely blank or anything — I can still do things, and sometimes I can notice something pleasant.\n\nBut that muted feeling is kind of the default right now. The part of me that used to reach for people, or feel something from my work, yoga, galleries, journaling… it’s gone very quiet. So I’m mostly just going through what I’m supposed to do.\n\nI hope that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: How many months have you been feeling this muted and going through the motions?\n\nPatient: It’s been… around eight months, I think. Not every single moment in the exact same way, but this overall change in how I’m thinking, working, and connecting with people has been there since we took my older relative into our apartment.\n\nI don’t want to overstate it, because I’ve still been managing, but yes — about eight months is probably the honest answer.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad most days?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it sad, exactly. I mean, some days I do feel low, but most of the time it’s more… muted, or flat, like I was saying.\n\nI don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. It’s not like I’m crying every day or feeling overwhelmed by sadness most of the day. It’s more that things don’t really reach me the way they used to. Is that a useful distinction?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad most of the day, even if it's not overwhelming?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not most of the day. I mean, there are moments where I feel down, yes, but I wouldn’t want to call it sadness all day if that makes sense.\n\nIt’s more like a low volume setting emotionally. Maybe that sounds evasive — I’m not trying to be. I just don’t notice sadness as the main thing most of the day.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel depressed most days?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d use the word depressed, at least not most days. Maybe that sounds like I’m splitting hairs, but it feels more accurate to say I’m kind of dulled or not fully engaged.\n\nI don’t want to minimize it if that’s what you’re asking about, but “depressed” feels a little stronger than what I notice day to day.\n\nDoctor: Would you describe your current emotional state as consistently feeling empty or hollow?\n\nPatient: Empty or hollow… maybe not in a dramatic way, but yes, somewhat. It’s like the part of me that used to naturally reach for people, or enjoy my work, or yoga, or going to galleries, even journaling… that part has gotten very quiet.\n\nI’m still doing what I’m supposed to do, mostly. I’m not falling apart. But I do feel like I’m moving through obligations more than actually wanting things or taking pleasure in them. Does that make sense as “empty,” or is that not quite what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad for most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’d say sad for most of the day nearly every day, no. I mean, I have patches where I feel low or kind of flat, but “sad most of the day” sounds stronger than what I’m aware of.\n\nI hope that’s a useful distinction. I’m not trying to be difficult — I just don’t want to overstate it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.
